*von gestern*German{}|Etymology}Literally:[of/from yesterday].|Adjective}#idiomatic outdated}•DVDs sind '''@^'''. Du brauchst Blu-Ray.}•DVDs are '''antiquated'''. You need Blu-ray.}#idiomatic born yesterday}•Ich bin doch nicht '''@^!'''}•I wasn't '''born yesterday!'''|Usage notes}The two senses are contradictory. Sense 1 is based on ''yesterday'' meaning “of the past” (and hence “obsolete”),whereas sense 2 is based on the same word meaning “recent” (and hence “inexperienced”). This may lead to some confusion,though rarely to actual misunderstandings.

^ö*Bonhase*German{}|Alternative forms}Böhnhase|Etymology}From Middle Low German bönhase,from Middle Low German tcat,from Middle Low German tloft,attic+from Middle Low German thare,the former being cognate of from German tstage. The word is first attested in the early 14th century as a personal name (and is thus probably even older). Since the 15th century it meant “illegally working craftsperson”. The sense “cat” is only attested later on,but there can be little doubt that it is the original meaning. Compare from German Dachhase,from English roof rabbit,etc.,which in fact must be loan translations of the Low German unless there were some ancient source for all of them. It was formerly supposed that the illegal workers were called  as a pun on from Ancient Greek tworker in an ignoble trade,whence later from German Banause,but this is highly unlikely since Greek studies were only just being revived in the 15th century. More probably these workers were simply compared to stray cats who holed up in lofts and fled when chased (as they commonly were by the members of the official guilds).|Pronunciation}/ˈbøːnˌhaːzə/}^Ⓓ1/14/De-^@.ogg|Noun}(mask.)}#North Germany cat; roof rabbit}•synononym:Dachhase}#North Germany A skilled manual worker or craftsperson who works illegally,originally without the concession of the respective guild.}#North Germany botcher; bungler Literally:[incompetent worker]}•synononym:[Pfuscher],[Stümper]

Cognate with from German Low German -ken,from German Low German -ke as well as from Dutch -tje and from Dutch -ken. More at -kin,(English).|Pronunciation}/çən/+çən+çn̩}The use of a syllabic nasal IPAchar:n̩ is less common in this ending than otherwise. The vowel tends to be preserved and often fronted,yielding IPAchar:çɪn.}/jən/,qqvariant,especially after fricatives like s. Never after a vowel or sonorant.}Ⓓ4/4e/De-@^.ogg|Suffix}#nowadays the most common suffix to create a diminutive form}•Hund}•Backe}•Nicker|Usage notes}Words ending in the suffix ''@^'' are always neuter,whatever the gender of the basic word.}Generally,with few exceptions,diminutives with ''@^'' have an umlauted stem vowel. Double vowels must be singled as the combinations ''ää'' and ''öö'' are not permitted in modern German orthography (e.g. Härchen from Haar,Bötchen from Boot).}Word-final ''-e'' and ''-en'' are lost before the diminutive ending:Kärtchen from Karte,Gärtchen from Garten.}Words ending in ''-ch'' (and some others) use the double suffix -elchen,e.g. Strichelchen from Strich. However,the suffix -lein is more common for these words in writing (Strichlein).}The plural diminutive is generally unchanged,but some words with a plural in -er may carry this ending over to the plural diminutive,yielding -erchen. This is standard in Kindchen → Kinderchen; most other forms (like Lämmerchen,etc.) are slightly informal or dated.|Derived terms}suffixsee:de|See also}-lein}-erl}-i

*Elchtest*German{}|Etymology}From Elch,coined by the German press in 1997 after a Mercedes-Benz had overturned at 60 km/h (less than 40 mph) during the Swedish standard test simulating an evasive maneuver. *Elen*German{}|Alternative forms}obsolete|Etymology}From Middle High German eln,elent (mid-13th c.). Usually derived from Old Lithuanian elenis (“deer”,modern from Lithuanian élnias),from Proto-Indo-European ⋆h₁el-. This derivation is supported by the fact that the from Middle High German el(e)n form appears during a time when the elk was gradually dying out in the German-speaking lands. However,it must be noted that the Lithuanian word does not refer to the elk specifically. Even more importantly,from Middle High German elen (from Middle High German elhen by regular loss of prevocalic ''h'' in Central and Low German dialects) can represent a generalised oblique case or plural of from Proto-Germanic ⋆elhô,weak-stem variant of from Proto-Germanic ⋆elhaz. This would make it a mere byform of from German notext1.|Pronunciation}/ˈeːlən/|Noun}n:m)}#archaic moose,Eurasian elk Literally:[taxfmt]}•synononym:Elch|Derived terms}@^antilope}@^tier

*Halloween*German{}|Etymology}English @^.|Pronunciation}/ˈhɛloˌwiːn/+/-ˌviːn/}Ⓓ9/93/De-@^.ogg|Noun}(neut.,plural:[@^(s)],strong)}#@^,(English)
*Halve Hahn*German{}|Etymology}Borrowed ksh. The origin is possibly an order like Can I get half a sandwich?,the last two words of which were then re-interpreted (either by foreigners or jokingly) as ''halve Hahn''.|Pronunciation}/ˌhalvəˈhaːn/|Noun}#regional a cheese sandwich,cut in half|Usage notes}The expression is rare in actual usage. Even in thick Ripuarian dialect one generally uses the plain and simple word m:Kiesbrütche ("cheese sandwich"). However,''@^'' is occasionally used as a shibboleth among Rhineland people and can be found on the menus of traditional Colognian pubs (as a humorous knowledge test for foreigners).}The term is either uninflected,e.g. (accusative) ''den @^'',or the first part behaves like a normal German adjective,thus ''den @-5'''n''' Hahn'',and so on.

*Harn*German{}|Etymology}From Middle High German - and from Old High German harn,from the from Proto-Indo-European - root from Proto-Indo-European dung,filth. Cognate with from Lithuanian tlye (for semantic connection,compare from English lant). *heben*German{}|Etymology}From Middle High German @^,from Middle High German heven (rarer from Middle High German heffen),from Old High German heffen,from Old High German heven,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆habbjan. Compare from Dutch heffen,from English heave,from Danish hæve. from German kapieren.}The ''-b-'' is regular in the past tense and participle. In Middle High German the paradigm was regularised by spreading ''-b-'' to the present tense; reinforced in East Central German by the local shift ''-v-'' → ''-b-'' (as in from East Central German Oben for Ofen). The fricative is preserved in related Hefe. The original past forms hub,gehaben were retained until Early Modern German. The verb was then shifted to the class of fliegen etc. (Compare from Dutch heffen,which was also shifted,though to a different ablaut class.)|Pronunciation}/ˈheːbən/+ˈheːbm̩}Ⓓ1/14/De-@^.ogg}Ⓓ3/39/De-@^2.ogg|Verb}@^(hob:hubarchaic,gehoben,höbe:hübearchaic)}#transitive to lift; to raise}#transitive to heave; to hoist}#reflexive to rise; to lift|Derived terms}ab@^}an@^}auf@^}aus@^}be@^}darunter@^}drüber@^}ein@^}empor@^}ent@^}er@^}Gewicht@^}Hebebühne}Heben}herauf@^}heraus@^}herüber@^}herunter@^}hervor@^}hinauf@^}hinaus@^}hinein@^}hinüber@^}hinweg@^}hoch@^}über@^}überheblich}um@^}unter@^}ver@^}weg@^}zurück@^

*Knofi*German{}|Etymology}A diminutive form pertaining to Knoblauch. The ''-f-'' is due to its being derived from dialectal forms. One would expect a from German Low German - origin (cf. from Middle Low German knoflōk),but there are also forms with ''-f-'' in some southern German dialects (themselves from Old High German chlovalouh). Southern origin is more likely given the regional form Knofel,but Low German would have helped to spread the form in the north.|Pronunciation}/ˈknoːfi/}/ˈknoːvi/,(less common)|Noun}(mask.)}#colloquial garlic}•synononym:[Knoblauch<g:m)],[;],[Knofel (southern Germany],[ Austria)<g:m)]

*-lein*German{}|Alternative forms}-el}-li}-lin}-elein|Etymology}From Middle High German -elīn,from Middle High German -līn,from Old High German -ilī,from Old High German -ilīn,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆-ilīn. Cognate to from Dutch -lijn.|Pronunciation}/laɪ̯n/}^Ⓓd/d5/LL-Q188_%28deu%29-Frank_C._Müller-@^.wav|Suffix}#suffix used to create a diminutive form; e.g. ''Kind'' → ''Kind'''lein'''''|Usage notes}In contemporary Standard German,is usual with words whose stems end in ''-ch'' or ''-g''; thus more often Bächlein than Bächelchen. There is a limited number of other forms that remain in common use,but for the most part  now has a poetic and archaic tone. The general diminutive suffix in the written language and in northern speech is -chen,while southern informal use prefers regional suffixes like -le,-li (Swabian/Alemannic) and -erl (Austro-Bavarian).}Diminutives in  generally have umlaut. The endings  are all deleted before the suffix.}The plural remains unchanged. However,a few nouns with a plural in -er may carry the same ending over to the plural diminutive,and a few diminutives referring to people allow a plural in . This is common with Kindlein →  and Fräulein → . Most other such forms are rare.|Derived terms}suffixsee:de|See also}-chen}-erl}-i

^ö*rontgen*German{}|Etymology}From the noun Röntgen,with -en reinterpreted as the infinitive ending of a verb.|Pronunciation}/ˈrœntɡən/+/ˈrœntçən/}^Ⓓ3/3e/De-^@.ogg|Verb}#to X-ray}•Gepäckstück '''geröntgt'''. Gepäckstück nicht geöffnet.}•Piece of baggage X-rayed. Piece of baggage not opened. (Tag found on baggage on returning from Germany.)|Usage notes}The unusual stop cluster in this word is often simplified to having a fricative instead,producing a pronunciation like IPAchar:ʁœntç-. This simplification is not limited to those (northern and central German) accents that feature or featured a merger of ''g'' and ''ch'' in initial or coda position. In the dialects which do feature a merger of final ''-g'' and ''-ch'',this pronunciation of ''^@'' is also not necessarily limited to forms where the cluster is in the coda.

*Schanze*German{}|Etymology}Of from German nocap1 origin. Attested since the 15th century. Several etymologies have been proposed.}Pfeifer suggests the regional Franconian/Hessian term from Rhine Franconian tbasket (itself of unknown origin) as the origin,arguing that entrenchments resemble baskets from the inside,being dug-out earth pits that have been reinforced from the inside with brushwood. *Sekt*German{}|Alternative forms}obsolete|Etymology}Borrowed in the 17th century,and provided with a paragogic ''-t'',from fr,which is also the original sense in German. The sense “sparkling wine” is believed to originate from an anecdote in 19th-century Berlin. The actor Ludwig Devrient supposedly ordered a bottle of wine using the phrase “from German Bring er mir @^,Schurke!”,based on the German translation of the line “Give me a cup of sack,(English),rogue!” from Shakespeare’s ''Henry IV''. He was served sparkling wine,his usual order,and this sense was given to the word  when the phrase and anecdote caught on.|Pronunciation}/zɛkt/}Ⓓ7/79/De-@^.ogg}säckt |Noun}(mask.,plural:[@^(e)s],diminituve:[@^chen])}#sparkling wine defdate:from 19th c.}•synononym:[Schaumwein (formal; officialese)],[;],[Prickelbrause  (colloquial; jokingly)],[Puffbrause (derogatory)],[Nuttenbrause (derogatory)]}#}• Was in diesen Wein- und @^gütern passiert  ist ein Wandel von genügsamen und oft auch etwas zuckrig schmeckenden '''@^en''' der Vergangenheit hin zu feinen  eleganten  oft knochentrockenen  frischen und druckvollen '''@^en'''  die plötzlich international Aufsehen erregen.}#obsolete sack gl:light-colored dry wine from southern Europe,especially Spain defdate:from 17th c.}•synononym:Südwein}#}@^ Gib mir ein Glas '''@^''',Schurke! – Ist keine Tugend mehr auf Erden?}•Give me a cup of '''sack''',rogue! *sich*German{}|Etymology}From Middle High German @^,from Old High German sih,from Proto-Germanic ⋆sek. Compare from Yiddish זיך,from Dutch zich.}This pronoun was originally restricted to the accusative case,while simple personal pronouns were used in the dative. Dative use of from Middle High German @^ in from Middle High German - was restricted to northern dialects of Central German. In Early Modern German,a rare dative sir also occurred,formed by analogy with mir,dir. An obstacle to the generalisation of this form was the use of  in the plural,where there operated the conflicting analogy with the merged accusative/dative forms uns,euch.|Pronunciation}/zɪç/}Ⓓe/ea/De-@^.ogg}Ⓓ0/08/De-@^2.ogg}Rhymes:ɪç|Pronoun}#reflexive pronoun Reflexive pronoun of the third person singular:herself,himself,themself,itself,oneself (''direct'' or ''indirect object'')}#reflexive pronoun Reflexive pronoun of the third person plural:themselves (''direct'' or ''indirect object'')

*Sitz im Leben*German{}|Etymology}Coined by Hermann Gunkel in 1930. Literally:[seat in life].|Noun}Sitz<m> im Leben,strong)}#idiomatic sociological setting|Usage notes}Though sometimes rendered by phrases like "life setting","situation in life" etc. the German term ''@^'' is better translated by "sociological setting" or left untranslated. As a technical term,it refers to the social setting of a text,particularly in the Bible. It is primarily used today in academic contexts,especially theology and biblical studies.

*Straßenbahn*German{}|Etymology}From @-5.|Pronunciation}/ˈʃtraːsənˌbaːn/+ˈʃtʀaːsn̩ˌbaːn+ˈʃtʀaːsənˌbaːn+ˈʃtraːsn̩ˌbaːn+ˈʃtraːsənˌbaːn}^Ⓓ2/24/De-@^.ogg|Noun}(fem.)}#streetcar,tramway}•synononym:[Elektrische (dated)],[;],[Tram (Austria],[ Switzerland)],[;],[Trambahn],[;],[Tramway (Austria)],[;],[Straba (Saxony — Würzburg)]|Usage notes}An urban train might drive on the streets on paths that are also used by cars,or it might move in tracks separated from the rest of the road traffic in an open-air area called Gleiskörper,and it might drive underground in tunnels. It depends on the city whether the very same trains drive on all three tracks or whether there are different trains for the three types of tracks. In the latter case,as in Berlin,one w:distinguishes the three types by name,calling the one driving with the cars ''@^'',the one on exclusive tracks S-Bahn,the ones serving the subway U-Bahn. Whereas in Cologne,(English) there is an indistinct @^,U-Bahn or w:Stadtbahn,the w:S-Bahn being a means of transportation of greater distance to connect the whole urban area with its multiple cities. But in places where there isn’t such a tripartite system and a single train moves between tracks used by cars,exclusive s-train-tracks and tunnels in a single route,it is called @^ indistinctly,or also Stadtbahn,this last especially by the officials themselves,so in Bielefeld,the terms S-Bahn and U-Bahn being uncommon,the city being w:surrounded by rural areas otherwise only having busses for local traffic. Whereas in Hamburg “w:@^” is a historical term since the @^en proper have been abolished completely by 1978.|Descendants}only in Germany itself

*Topf*German{}ein '''@^'''|Etymology}From Middle High German topf,from Old High German ⋆topf,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆dopp,from Proto-Germanic thollow,bowl,from Proto-Indo-European tdeep,hollow. Cognate with from Lukpa tlittle cup,thimble,from Low German tshell,pod,bowl,from Saterland Frisian tshell,pod,bowl,from Lukpa tcup in which a diamond is cut.}Both in historic sources and modern dialects,the word is almost entirely restricted to East Central German (generally as from East Central German Topp,in the very south as from East Central German @^). West Central German only shows a neuter derivative in from Middle High German düppen (whence e.g. from Luxembourgish Dëppen). Nevertheless it must have existed in Old High German as the a-mutation assures inheritance. Otherwise it is declined like a normal adjective:''das @^'''e''' Geld''.}In the plural,the adjective is usually declined even without a preceding article or determiner:''@^e Kinder''. However,it may be left unchanged when modified by a preceding adverb,e.g. in the combinations wie ''@^'' (“how many”) and so ''@^'' (“so many”):''wie @^ Kinder'' or ''wie @^e Kinder''.}The comparative form mehr is invariable and never declined; it cannot be preceded by any article or determiner (note however mehrere,mehreres,and obsolete mehre,mehres). The superlative meist is declined like a normal adjective.}<!--|Adverb}de-adv:meisten}#much,a lot}•Wir haben '''@^''' gelacht.}•We laughed a lot.|Derived terms}allzu@^}so@^}wie@^|See also}manch||Pennsylvania German{}|Etymology}from Proto-Indo-European ⋆pleh₁-}From Middle High German vile,from Old High German many,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆felu,from Proto-Germanic ⋆felu,from Proto-Indo-European ⋆pleh₁-. *wayne*German{}|Etymology}From English Wayne,which sounds somewhat similar to German wen (“whom”,pronounced veːn or ʋeːn). The sense is derived from trick questions of the sort:„''Kennst du Wayne?''“ – ? – „''Wayne interessiert’s.''“,that is:wen interessiert's. (“Do you know Wayne?” – ? – “Wayne cares.”  “Who cares.”).|Pronunciation}/wɛɪ̯n/+/weːn/|Interjection}#youth who cares}•synononym:[mir doch egal],[wen interessiert's]}•Helmut Schmidt ist tot. — Ach,'''@^'''.}•w:Helmut Schmidt}•Helmut Schmidt is dead. — Ah,'''who cares'''.

*werden*German{}|Etymology}From Middle High German wërden,from Old High German werdan,from Proto-West Germanic ⋆werþan,from Proto-Germanic ⋆werþaną. Cognate with from Dutch worden,obsolete from English worth,from Swedish varda,from Norwegian Nynorsk verta,and also with from Latin to turn.}The use as a passive auxiliary is old and found throughout West Germanic, whereas the use as a future auxiliary is a Middle High German innovation. It originated in inchoative constructions with the present participle:. Since the 14th century,the participle was increasingly replaced with the infinitive,probably by analogy with the older future auxiliaries wollen and sollen. *Vorsicht*German{}|Alternative forms}obsolete|Etymology}Chiefly a backformation from v1-Ĭig,from Middle High German vorsihtec,from Middle High German vürsihtec,from Old High German foresihtig,derived from Old High German foresiht,a from Latin nocap1 and from Latin provisio. from German Sicht. from German Vorsehung. Although the noun must underlie the adjective,the former is not attested again (after Notker) before the 15th century; it remained rare until ca. 1650.  Compare from Dutch voorzicht,from English foresight.|Pronunciation}/ˈfoːʁˌzɪçt/+ˈfoːɐ̯ˌzɪçt+ˈfɔɐ̯-}Ⓓe/ee/De-@^.ogg}Vor+sicht|Noun}(fem.)}#caution,cautiousness}#dated precaution,providence,provision}•synononym:[Vorsorge],[Vorkehrung]|Derived terms}@^smaßnahme}v1-Ĭshalber|Interjection}#beware!,caution!,careful!

*Zettel*German{}ein ''@^''.|Pronunciation}/ˈt͡sɛtəl/+ˈt͡sɛtl̩}Zet+tel|Etymology 1}Early Modern High German zeddel,zedel,from Middle High German zedele,from Middle High German zedel,a loan from Italian cedola,from German cedula,from Latin schedula,the diminutive of from Latin scheda,from Latin tstrip of papyrus ultimately from Ancient Greek tsplinter,fragment. Cognate to from Low German Zeddel,Palatine from Rhine Franconian Zeddel,from English schedule.}The spelling with ''-tt-'' was found from the 15th century in Upper German; the spelling with ''-dd-'' persisted until the 19th century,primarily in authors from Central or Northern Germany. Luther mostly writes ''zedel'',Goethe alternates between ''zeddel'' and ''zettel''. The (now obsolete) spelling variant ''zettul'' is influenced by from French cédule.}Occasional weak inflection was found in Middle High German and persisted into the 18th century. The original feminine gender was retained until Luther's time,but occasional masculine or neuter gender was found by the late Middle High German period. The masculine gender has predominated since the 19th century.|Alternative forms}zedel}zeddel}ziddel}zöttel}zättel}zettul}Zeddel}Zettul|Noun}(mask.,diminituve:[@^chen])}#a small or loose piece of paper,slip}#}@^ der schutzengel ... sträuet etliche '''zettele''' nider,darauf schöne sprüch aus heiliger schrift wider die hoffart}•the guardian angel scatters a number of loose pages from above,on which are fine sayings from the holy writ against the sin of pride}#note,message,letter}#}'''1511''',Albrecht von Eyb,''Spiegel der Sitten'',Augsburg,:}#}: name er sollich '''zedel''' der klagen,verprennet sy und sprach+he Constantine took such letters of complaint against the bishops,burned them,and said ...}#}@^ Gut. Ich bekam einen '''@^''':ich sollte um ein Uhr in der Salpêtrère sein. Ich war dort.}#poster,placard,public notice}#}'''1725''',Chr. Thomasius,''Gedanken und Erinnerungen'',Marburg,3,104:}#}:ohnerachtet herr d. Pf. meinen '''zettul''' vom schwartzen brette hatte abreiszen lassen+nevertheless Mr. d. Pf. had my poster taken down from the university's bulletin board|Derived terms}Beipackzettel}Denkzettel}Handzettel}Kassenzettel}Klebezettel}Lohnzettel}Lottozettel}Meldezettel}Merkzettel}Notizzettel}Packzettel}Schmierzettel}Schummelzettel}Spickzettel}Totenzettel}Waschzettel}Wunschzettel}@^kasten}@^wirtschaft|Etymology 2}A technical term in weaving recorded from the later 15th century (in Middle High German in the compound from Middle High German zettelgarn).}The noun is derived from the verb zetten via the suffix -el denoting tools (as in Hebel,Stößel). The verb ''zetten'' itself is from Proto-Germanic tto strew,scatter,whence also from English tdung.}In German translations of Shakespeare's ''A Midsummer Night's Dream'' the name of the weaver Nick Bottom (one of the “rude mechanicals,” from glossball or skein of thread replaced by a German technical term of weaving following Christoph Martin Wieland,1762).|Noun}(mask.)}#weaving warp}•synononym:[Kettfaden],[Werft]}#}• kainen '''zettel''' innemen oder wúrken  er hab in dann selbs gezettlet (he should take over or work no warp)  unless that he has himself warped it}#}@^ wenn ... an einem kleid eines aussatzes maal sein wirt,es sey ... am '''zettel''' oder am eintrag|tThe garment also that the plague of leprosy is in,whether ... it be in the warp,or woof ...}#}@^ In den Niederlanden hatte das Teppichwirken mit stehendem '''@^''',Hautelisse genannt,sich schon auf den höchsten Grad erhoben.|Derived terms}zetteln

*man ist, was man isst*German{}|Pronunciation}Ⓓ0/05/De-man_ist%2C_was_man_isst.ogg|Proverb}#you are what you eat}#}|year1885|textSchlecht genährte Völker sind auch willensschlaffe und niedrig stehende Völker. Der bekannte Spruch:„'''Man ist,was man isst'''“,gilt für eine grosse Gesammtheit ganz so,wie für das Individuum.|pageurlhttps://books.google.com/books?idfMMLAAAAYAAJ&pgPA157|page157|tPoorly-fed people are also weak-willed and lowly people. The common saying "'''you are what you eat'''" applies just as much to the larger community as it does to the individual.}#}• „'''Man ist  was man isst'''!“ Vielleicht ist ein bisschen Wahres daran. In der Makrobiotik geht man sogar so weit  dass man sagt  man sieht aus wie ein Schwein  wenn man zu viel Schweinefleisch verzehrt. ("'''You are what you eat'''!" Maybe there's some truth to it. In the macrobiotic diet)  they even go so far as to say that you look like a pig if you eat too much pork.}#}• '''Man ist  was man isst'''. Wenn wir Fleisch von Tieren zu uns nehmen  müssen wir uns klar darüber sein  dass der Preis dafür die Tötung eines Lebewesens ist. ('''You are what you eat'''. When we eat meat from animals)  we must understand  that the price of this is the killing of a living being.

*Ungeziefer*German{}|Etymology}From early modern German ,,a variant form of from Middle High German ungezibere. These pertain to from Old High German sacrificial animal and hence originally meant “animals unsuitable for sacrifice”,ultimately from Proto-Germanic offering,sacrifice,victim. The word is rarely attested in medieval texts due to suppression of words reminiscent of heathen practices,but must have survived in lower registers.|Pronunciation}/ˈʊnɡəˌt͡siːfɐ/+/ˈʊŋɡə-/}Ⓓ9/9d/De-@^.ogg|Noun}(neut.)}#uncountable vermin; pests; ''restricted to small animals,usually ''insects'' or ''spiders,'' sometimes also ''mice'',etc.''}•Schädling}•Der Küchenschrank saß voller Motten und dieses '''@^''' war in alle Dosen und Tüten eingedrungen.}•The kitchen cupboard was full of moths and these '''vermin''' had got into all containers and bags.}#countable a vermin animal; a despised creature}#}@^ :translatorw:Willa Muir|translation_year1933|passageAls Gregor Samsa eines Morgens aus unruhigen Träumen erwachte,fand er sich in seinem Bett zu einem ungeheuren '''@^''' verwandelt.|translationAs Gregor Samsa awoke one morning from uneasy dreams he found himself transformed in his bed into an enormous '''insect'''.

*Schweizer*German{}|Alternative forms}Schweitzer|Etymology}from Middle High German swīzer. Equivalent to @-2+er. The senses "doorman" and "milker" arose because Swiss people were commonly employed in Germany in those professions; the sense "sacristan" arose because sacristans' garb resembled that of the Swiss Guards. For the sense development compare also from French Swiss:from French doorman.|Pronunciation}/ˈʃvaɪ̯t͡sɐ/}Ⓓ6/69/De-@^.ogg}Rhymes:aɪ̯t͡sɐ}Schwei+zer|Noun}(mask.) feminine form:Swiss (female) or melker (female)}#Swiss person,Swiss (male)}•synononym:Eidgenosse}•Mitteleuropäer}#Swiss Guard}•@^garde}#Catholicism sacristan}•synononym:Küster}#milker gl:a person or man who milks cows}#doorman|Hypernyms}Mitteleuropäer|Hyponyms}Auslandsschweizer}Deutschschweizer}Innerschweizer}Nordschweizer}Ostschweizer}Papierschweizer}Südschweizer}Welschschweizer}Westschweizer}Zentralschweizer}Aargauer}Baseler}Berner}Genfer}Graubündner}Luzerner}Neuenburger}Tessiner}Thurgauer}Zürcher|Derived terms}@^in}schweizerisch|Descendants}obsolete}desctree:bor1|Proper noun}surname)}#surname|Adjective}#relational of Switzerland|Derived terms}@^heit
